}}

(106)

This is given by

\displaystyle\Gamma_{N}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int d\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt\nu\,d\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt^{d}q\,\frac{iV_{1}^{\prime}(q,k_{1})}{\nu+\omega_{1}-\Omega({\mathbf{q}}+{\mathbf{k}}_{1})^{*}}\cdot\frac{iV_{2}^{\prime}(q+k_{1},k_{2})}{\nu+\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}-\Omega({\mathbf{q}}+{\mathbf{k}}_{1}+{\mathbf{k}}_{2})^{*}}\cdots\frac{iV_{N}^{\prime}(q-k_{N},k_{N})}{\nu-\Omega({\mathbf{q}})^{*}},

(107)

where we have used the fact that
\mathcal{G}_{\psi\phi}=\mathcal{G}_{\phi\psi}^{*}
. Now, all of the poles are in the lower half-plane. Again, if
N\geq 2
and there are fewer than
N-2
factors of frequency appearing in the vertices in total, then, we can always close the contour in the upper half-plane, thereby proving that
\Gamma_{N}^{\prime}=0
.

Although
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}
do not necessarily vanish separately, it turns out that their sum, given by

\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}=\int d\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt\nu\,d\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt^{d}q\biggl(\frac{-iV(-q)}{\nu-\Omega({\mathbf{q}})}+\frac{iV(q)}{\nu-\Omega({\mathbf{q}})^{*}}\biggr).

(108)

does indeed vanish. This follows from the fact that the vertices (
99
) are linear in momentum and purely imaginary,
V(-q)=-V(q)
and
[-iV(-q)]^{*}=iV(q)
, so that
\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}=\Gamma_{1}^{*}
, and

\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}=2i\int d\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt^{d}q\,V(q)\int d\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt\nu\,\frac{\nu-\text{Re}\,\Omega({\mathbf{q}})}{(\nu-\text{Re}\,\Omega({\mathbf{q}}))^{2}+(\text{Im}\Omega({\mathbf{q}}))^{2}}.

(109)

One can change the integration variable to the shifted frequency
\tilde{\nu}=\nu-\text{Re}\,\Omega({\mathbf{q}})
. Provided
\text{Im}\,\Omega({\mathbf{q}})\neq 0
, the numerator is odd in
\tilde{\nu}
while the denominator is even, and so the integral vanishes, so that

\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}=\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{1}^{*}=0.

(110)

Therefore, the nonrenormalization theorem applies to the case
N=1
as well as
N\geq 2
.

Let us momentarily relax the isotropic assumption so that
\kappa
splits into
\kappa_{x}
and
\kappa_{y}
in each direction, respectively
15
15

15

We note that this is not to be understood in terms of the more general model for Malthusian flocks referenced in
9
;
13
;
10
;
12
;
11
, where additional derivatively-coupled operators appear with coefficients that vanish in the limit
\kappa_{x}\to\kappa_{y}
. Rather, we consider the deformation of the isotropic diffusion term alone so that it can have differing
\kappa_{x}
and
\kappa_{y}
.
. Assign the following
bare
dimensions:

\displaystyle[t]

\displaystyle=-z,

\displaystyle[x]

\displaystyle=-\zeta,

\displaystyle[y]

\displaystyle=-1,

\displaystyle[{\cal L}]

\displaystyle=1+\zeta+z.

(111)

The form of the free Lagrangian (
16a
) then implies that:

\displaystyle 1+\zeta+z

\displaystyle=[\phi]+z+[\psi]=[\phi]+[\kappa_{x}]+2\zeta+[\psi]=[\phi]+[\kappa_{y}]+2+[\psi]=[\lambda]+[\psi]+[\phi]+\zeta

\displaystyle=[\Delta]+2[\phi]=[\lambda]+[\phi]+1,

(112)

which are equivalent to the relations

\displaystyle[\phi]+[\psi]

\displaystyle=1+\zeta,\hskip 50.0pt[\kappa_{x}]=z-2\zeta,

(113a)

\displaystyle[\lambda]

\displaystyle=z-\zeta,\hskip 50.0pt[\kappa_{y}]=z-2,

(113b)

\displaystyle[\Delta]+2[\phi]

\displaystyle=1+\zeta+z,\hskip 32.0pt[\lambda]+[\phi]=\zeta+z.

(113c)

Of course,
\psi
must be dimensionless in order to retain its interpretation as an angle, for otherwise, we would not be able to take its cosine or sine. This is of course, a direct corollary of the power-counting renormalizability of all
z=2
actions in 2+1 dimensions with up to four spatial derivatives
23
;
50
;
16
;
17
. Therefore,

[\psi]=0,

(114)

from which it follows that

\displaystyle[\phi]

\displaystyle=1+\zeta,\hskip 50.0pt[\kappa_{x}]=z-2\zeta,

(115a)

\displaystyle[\kappa_{y}]

\displaystyle=z-2,\hskip 50.0pt[\lambda]=z-\zeta,

(115b)

\displaystyle[\Delta]

\displaystyle=z-\zeta-1,\hskip 31.0pt[\lambda]=z-1.

(115c)

The last two equations of (
115b
) and (
115c
) then imply that

\zeta=1,

(116)

and so the dimensions
[x]
and
[y]
are identical, and

\displaystyle[\phi]

\displaystyle=2,\hskip 50.0pt[\kappa]=z-2,

(117a)

\displaystyle[\lambda]

\displaystyle=z-1,\hskip 32.0pt[\Delta]=z-2,

(117b)

where
[\kappa_{x}]=[\kappa_{y}]=[\kappa]
follows by corollary. At this point, it is argued within the context of a dynamical renormalization group analysis in
13
that in order to keep
\kappa_{x}
,
\kappa_{y}
and
\Delta
fixed, or, in other words, to make them dimensionless, we have to pick

z=2.

(118)

It follows immediately that
[\lambda]=1
and so
\lambda
is a
relevant
parameter at bare level.

A.2
Organization of the Diagrammatic Expansion

Define the following quantities:

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=\#\ \text{of loops},

(119a)

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=\#\ \text{of internal lines},

(119b)

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle=\#\ \text{of external lines},

(119c)

\displaystyle V_{2n+1}

\displaystyle=\#\ \text{of $(2n+1)$-vertices},

(119d)

\displaystyle V_{2n+2}

\displaystyle=\#\ \text{of $(2n+2)$-vertices}.

(119e)

There are two simple relations one can immediately derive:

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=I-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(V_{2n+1}+V_{2n+2})+1,

(120a)

\displaystyle 2I+E

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\bigl[(2n+1)V_{2n+1}+(2n+2)V_{2n+2}\bigr].

(120b)

The first equation comes from the fact that every internal line gets its own momentum to be integrated over, but each vertex imposes one momentum conservation relation among those momenta, except that those relations are themselves not all independent as there is one relation among them for overall momentum conservation.

The second equation simply counts the total number of endpoints of lines that must be attached on all of the vertices. Every vertex of type
V_{2n+1}
has
2n+1
lines and every vertex of type
V_{2n+2}
has
2n+2
lines. These must be exhausted by all of the internal and external lines, except that internal lines contribute both of their endpoints while external lines only contribute one.

A.2.1
Corrections to the Propagators

For propagators,
E=2
and we can write

I=-1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\biggl((2n+1)\frac{V_{2n+1}}{2}+(n+1)V_{2n+2}\biggr),

(121)

and thus,

L=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\biggl((2n-1)\frac{V_{2n+1}}{2}+nV_{2n+2}\biggr).

(122)

Clearly, the sum of all the
V_{2n+1}
coefficients must be even. This is because each
2n+1
vertex comes with an odd number of fields, and given that they must be pairwise contracted in order to form a diagram, there is no way this can be done if the total number of fields is odd. Therefore we need the total number of such
2n+1
vertices, i.e. the sum of all the
V_{2n+1}
to be an even number.

A.2.2
Corrections to the Vertices

The corrections to the
(2n+2)
-vertex will have
E=2n+2
, so that

I=-(n+1)+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\biggl((2m+1)\frac{V_{2m+1}}{2}+(m+1)V_{2m+2}\biggr),

(123)

and

L=-n+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\biggl((2m-1)\frac{V_{2m+1}}{2}+mV_{2m+2}\biggr).

(124)

Note that (
121
) and (
122
) are simply the
n=0
versions of (
123
) and (
124
), respectively. As with the propagator corrections, the sum of the
V_{2m+1}
coefficients must be even.

On the other hand, the corrections to
V_{2n+1}
have
E=2n+1
, and so

I=-\biggl(n+\frac{1}{2}\biggr)+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\biggl((2m+1)\frac{V_{2m+1}}{2}+(m+1)V_{2m+2}\biggr),

(125)

and

L=-n+\frac{1}{2}+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\biggl((2m-1)\frac{V_{2m+1}}{2}+mV_{2m+2}\biggr).

(126)

In this case, the sum of the
V_{2m+1}
’s must be odd.

A.3

For the one-loop corrections to the propagators,
L=1
in (
122
) implies that
V_{\geq 5}=0
, and so

1=\frac{V_{3}}{2}+V_{4}.

(127)

The possible values of
V_{3}
and
V_{4}
are tabulated in Table
1
along with the structure of the corresponding diagram. The diagram form merely shows the structure of the diagram; it does not show the dashed lines for
\phi
.

).

For the one-loop corrections to the
(2n+2)
-vertex, we set
L=1
in (
124
) and find that the sum must terminate at
m=n
, so that the equation simplifies to

2n+2=V_{3}+2V_{4}+3V_{5}+4V_{6}+\cdots+(2n+1)V_{2n+3}+(2n+2)V_{2n+4}.

(128)

This is complicated to analyze for general
n
. However, the stucture of diagrams is fairly easy to see for specific values of
n
and essentially follows the same pattern as the
n=1
case. The case
n=1
, corresponding to corrections to the 4-point vertex, gives

4=V_{3}+2V_{4}+3V_{5}+4V_{6}.

), we find that the sum must terminate at

A.4
Superficial Degree of Divergence

The power-counting classification presented in sec.
A.1
has important consequences for the superficial degree of divergence of the various Feynman diagrams. A diagram with a
\psi
-
\psi
propagator in an internal line will always have a lower degree of divergence compared to a diagram with the same structure, but where the
\psi
-
\psi
propagator is replaced with either a
\phi
-
\psi
or a
\psi
-
\phi
propagator. In fact, the degree of divergence is reduced by 2 for each internal
\phi
-
\psi
or
\psi
-
\phi
propagator replaced with a
\psi
-
\psi
propagator, and further reduced by 1 because one vertex factor will now contain an external rather than an internal momentum factor.

Let us compute the superficial degree of divergence for arbitrary
z
and spatial dimension
d
before plugging in
z=d=2
. A diagram with
L
loops will have
L
internal frequency-momentum integrals for a total dimension of
(z+d)L
. Each internal line comes with a factor of a propagator. Let us split
I
up into the number of
\phi
-
\psi
or
\psi
-
\phi
internal lines and the number of
\psi
-
\psi
internal lines:

I=I_{\phi\psi}+I_{\psi\psi}.

(132)

Each internal line comes with a corresponding propagator factor, which has dimension
-z
for a
\phi
-
\psi
or
\psi
-
\phi
line and dimension
-2z
for a
\psi
-
\psi
line. Furthermore, each
I_{\phi\psi}
contributes one factor of internal momentum due to the vertex. Therefore, the superficial degree of divergence,
D
, of any diagram is

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=(z+d)L-z(I_{\phi\psi}+2I_{\psi\psi})+I_{\phi\psi}

\displaystyle=(z+d)L-2zI_{\psi\psi}-(z-1)I_{\phi\psi}

\displaystyle=(z+d)L-2zI+(z+1)I_{\phi\psi}.

(133)

Plugging in
z=d=2
gives

D=4(L-I)+3I_{\phi\psi}.

(134)

Indeed, if we reduce the number of internal
\phi
-
\psi
lines by one, so that
I_{\phi\psi}\to I_{\phi\psi}-1
, then the superficial degree of divergence of the diagram is reduced by 3, as previously described.

Appendix B
One-Loop Order – Regularization and Scheme Independence of Log Divergences

The simple dimension counting of the previous subsections have very important consequences. For example, for one-loop diagrams, one has

D=4-4I+3I_{\phi\psi}.

(135)

The maximum value for
I_{\phi\psi}
is
I
, and so

D_{\rm max}=4-I.

(136)

We consider
I\geq 2
and
I=1
as separate cases. For
I\geq 2
, one has
D_{\rm max}\leq 2
, and so we need only consider the case
I_{\phi\psi}=I
, as reducing
I_{\phi\psi}
would reduce
D
by at least 3, thereby giving
D\leq-1
which correspond to UV-finite diagrams that do not require renormalization. The case
I=1
implies
D_{\rm max}=3
, where we could either have
I_{\phi\psi}=1
for which
D=3
, or
I_{\phi\psi}=0
, for which
D=0
(i.e., corresponding to a log-divergence). However, if
I_{\phi\psi}=I
, the process involves the sum of loop diagrams where
\phi
-
\psi
and
\psi
-
\phi
lines are interchanged, and therefore vanishes according to the non-renormalization theorem in sec.
A.1
. Hence, only one family of diagrams contributes to the renormalization of the parameters of our theory at one loop:
(I,I_{\phi\psi})=(1,0)
, which correspond to log-divergences.

For the
\phi
-
\psi
propagator, the relevant one loop diagram is thus given by:

\vbox{\hbox{
\hbox to91.85pt{\vbox to48.02pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 45.92458pt\lower-3.52257pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -62.99 0 L -32.6 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-23.16228pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.54 4.32 C -2.9 1.73 -1.45 0.5 0 0 C -1.45 -0.5 -2.9 -1.73 -3.54 -4.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 31.5 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 35.04 0 L 62.99 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{25.72227pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.54 4.32 C 2.9 1.73 1.45 0.5 0 0 C 1.45 -0.5 2.9 -1.73 3.54 -4.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{{{}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C -19.15 11.05 -22.11 56.69 -1.11 56.69}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-0.4pt}{40.97218pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.54 4.32 C -2.9 1.73 -1.45 0.5 0 0 C -1.45 -0.5 -2.9 -1.73 -3.54 -4.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{{{}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 19.15 11.05 22.11 56.69 0 56.69}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}}=M_{\phi\psi}^{(1)}.

(137)

Here, we are using the standard convention wherein the external propagators of the diagram are amputated
16
16

16

Although this convention arises from the necessities of LSZ reduction in quantum field theory
24
;
40
;
7
, it persists in statistical field theory in isolating the interacting parts of the one particle irreducible diagrams given the role of external propagators in simply tracking momentum in and outflow in a perturbative computation
21
;
44
.
. In so doing, this diagram is, in fact, the one loop correction to the inverse propagator, not the propagator itself. This is why we have denoted it by
M
, not
{\cal G}
. This evaluates to

\displaystyle-\Biggl\langle\psi_{-\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}\phi_{\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}\frac{1}{3!}\lambda\int_{\nu,{\mathbf{q}}}\phi_{\nu,{\mathbf{q}}}(iq_{x})\int_{1,2,3}\psi_{\nu_{1},{\mathbf{q}}_{1}}\psi_{\nu_{2},{\mathbf{q}}_{2}}\psi_{\nu_{3},{\mathbf{q}}_{3}}\,\delta\hskip-2.40005pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt\biggl(\nu+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\nu_{j}\biggr)\delta\hskip-2.40005pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt^{(d)}\biggl({\mathbf{q}}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}{\mathbf{q}}_{j}\biggr)\Biggr\rangle_{0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{i\lambda}{2}\biggl(\frac{-i}{\omega-\lambda k_{x}-i\kappa k^{2}}\biggr)k_{x}\biggl(\frac{-i}{\omega-\lambda k_{x}-i\kappa k^{2}}\biggr)\int_{\nu,{\mathbf{q}}}\frac{2\Delta}{(\nu-\lambda q_{x}-i\kappa q^{2})(\nu-\lambda q_{x}+i\kappa q^{2})}

\displaystyle=-i\lambda k_{x}\mathscr{I}\langle\psi_{-\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}\phi_{\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}\rangle_{0}^{2},

(138)

where

\mathscr{I}=\Delta\int_{\nu,{\mathbf{q}}}\frac{1}{(\nu-\lambda q_{x}-i\kappa q^{2})(\nu-\lambda q_{x}+i\kappa q^{2})}=\Delta\int_{\widetilde{\nu},{\mathbf{q}}}\frac{1}{\widetilde{\nu}^{2}+(\kappa q^{2})^{2}},

(139)

and

\widetilde{\nu}=\nu-\lambda q_{x}.

(140)

The
\widetilde{\nu}
integral is straightforward to compute either using the calculus of residues or a trigonometric substitution, and the result is

\mathscr{I}=\frac{\Delta}{2\kappa}\int_{{\mathbf{q}}}\frac{1}{q^{2}}\xrightarrow{d=2}\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dq}{q}\xrightarrow{\rm reg}\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}\ln\frac{\Lambda}{\mu},

(141)

where “reg” stands for “regularized,” implemented most directly with hard UV and IR cut-offs,
\Lambda
and
\mu
, respectively. Ideally, we should ensure that this would be the result in any scheme, given that the coefficients of the leading short distance logarithmic divergences dictate the corresponding coefficients of the beta functions, and therefore the renormalization group flow of the associated couplings.

Fortunately, the coefficients of leading short-distance logarithmic divergences are scheme-independent. That is, for any regularization scheme, whether hard cutoff, zeta-function, Pauli-Villars, point-split, dimensional regularization, etc., the leading log divergences can explicitly be shown to have the same coefficient (see e.g.
7
;
37
). This is an important enough (and often misunderstood) point, that is worth elaborating upon it in the present context.

One can, for example, arrive at the same coefficient for the leading logarithmic divergence in the context of dimensional regularization by first recasting the integral in (
141
) in terms of a related integral over
\mathbb{R}^{2}
:

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dq}{q}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{q\,dq}{q^{2}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{d^{2}q}{q^{2}},

(142)

and realizing that for integrals that include an explicit mass scale
m
, that:

I_{D}(m^{2})=\int\frac{d^{D}k}{(2\pi)^{D}}\frac{k^{2A}}{(k^{2}+m^{2})^{B}}=\frac{\Gamma\left(A+\frac{D}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(B-A-\frac{D}{2}\right)}{(4\pi)^{D/2}\Gamma\left(\frac{D}{2}\right)\Gamma(B)}\left(m^{2}\right)^{A-B+D/2}.

(143)

Although the integrand (
142
) is scaleless and therefore might appear to nominally vanish when dimensionally regularized, this vanishing can be traced to a cancellation of UV and IR contributions enforced by default in dimensional regularization. In order to see this, we expand the divergent integrand via partial fractions into the sum

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{d^{2}q}{q^{2}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d^{2}q\left[\frac{1}{q^{2}+m^{2}}+\frac{m^{2}}{q^{2}(q^{2}+m^{2})}\right],

(144)

where
m^{2}
arises as an auxiliary mass scale via the partial fraction expansion that subsequently cancels between the two contributions. Of particular note is the fact that the first term on the right hand side of the above is UV-divergent, whereas the second term is IR-divergent. Evaluating both terms using (
143
) and expanding around
D=2-\delta
dimensions results in equal and opposite contributions of the form

\pm\left[\frac{1}{\delta}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\log\frac{m^{2}}{4\pi\mu^{2}}+\gamma_{E}\right)\right],

(145)

where
\mu
is some arbitrary mass scale necessitated from dimensional deformation. Were we to label the UV and IR pole contributions separately, one arrives at the result

\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dq}{q}\xrightarrow{\rm reg}\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}\left[\frac{1}{\delta_{\rm UV}}-\frac{1}{\delta_{\rm IR}}+\log\frac{\mu_{\rm UV}}{\mu_{\rm IR}}\right],

(146)

which matches with (
141
) with the identifications
\mu_{\rm UV}\equiv\Lambda
and
\mu_{\rm IR}\equiv\mu
. Whatever scheme one works in, provided one is expanding around a background field that is a
stable saddle point
of the MSRDJ action, all IR divergences will cancel in well defined observables (and can be taken as the defining criteria for the latter)
7
;
37
. UV divergences get subtracted by appropriate counterterms, which, remarkably, can be absorbed via the renormalization of the couplings in the action (
16a
) and (
16b
), i.e., the theory is itself
renormalizable
in the particle physics sense, and does not need to be treated as an effective theory
7
. Given that it is the coefficient of the leading logarithmic short distance divergences that determine how couplings run with scale, we see that one would have arrived at the same running having implemented dimensional regularization properly, as one would have by naïvely imposing hard cut-offs.

An alternate manner in which one could have arrived at this same conclusion would have been through reexamining the statement that (
141
) legitimately corresponds to a scaleless integral. We note that implicit in the background field expansion invoked in our calculation is the fact that
\psi_{0}
and
\phi_{0}
in (
90
) correspond to stable saddle points of the MSRDJ action. Although taking both to be (vanishing) constants certainly meet this criteria, one will in reality have very long wavelength fluctuations of the background fields such that the net effect of expanding around them would be to generate an IR mass scale for the propagator in (
142
) arising from derivatives acting on the background fields
17
17

17

Note that when a similar integral is encountered, for example, in
40
in the context of the non-linear sigma model in 1+1 dimensions, an IR regulator is introduced by hand. Strictly speaking, this should be understood in terms of the argument just presented, with the net result that the one loop beta functions are proportional to the target space curvature felt by slow variations of the background field.
, which we denote as
\mu_{\rm IR}\equiv\mu_{\rm BG}
so that one instead has to evaluate:

I=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{d^{2}q}{q^{2}}\rightarrow\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{d^{2}q}{q^{2}+\mu_{\rm BG}^{2}},

(147)

which now only manifests a UV divergence. Regularizing the above using (
143
) results in

\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{d^{2}q}{q^{2}+\mu_{\rm BG}^{2}}\xrightarrow{\rm reg}\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}\left[\frac{1}{\delta_{\rm UV}}+\log\left(\frac{\mu_{\rm UV}}{\mu_{\rm BG}}\right)\right],

(148)

and will result in the same running as would have been extracted by hard cut-offs, or factorizing and dimensionally regularizing the nominally scaleless integral (
141
).

Thus satisfied by the scheme independence of the coefficient of the leading UV logarithmic divergence, we find that upon amputation of the external
\phi
-
\psi
propagators, the inverse propagator gets the following scale dependent one loop correction:

M_{\phi\psi}^{(1)}=-i\lambda k_{x}\mathscr{I}=-i\lambda k_{x}\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}\ln\frac{\Lambda}{\mu}.

(149)

Given that the bare value is
M_{\phi\psi}^{(0)}=\widetilde{O}_{\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}=-i(\omega-\lambda k_{x}+i\kappa k^{2})
, adding these contributions together results in

M_{\phi\psi}^{(0)}+M_{\phi\psi}^{(1)}=-i\bigl[\omega-(1-\mathscr{I})\lambda k_{x}+i\kappa k^{2}\bigr].

(150)

At this point, one might be tempted to conclude that the only quantity that gets renormalized is
\lambda
. As we elaborate upon later, this is incorrect. Instead,
\lambda
,
\Delta
,
\kappa
,
\phi
, and the spatial coordinates
{\mathbf{x}}
all receive anomalous dimensions, as given by (
180
) and elaborated upon in Appendix
D
.

We furthermore note that the correction to the
\phi
-
\psi
inverse propagator is the same as for the
\psi
-
\phi
case. In addition, no diagram of this type, that is with
(I,I_{\phi\psi})=(1,0)
, exists with both external fields being
\phi
fields. Therefore, there is no correction to
M_{\phi\phi}
, or in other words,
M_{\phi\phi}^{(1)}=0
and

M_{\phi\phi}^{(0)}+M_{\phi\phi}^{(1)}=M_{\phi\phi}^{(0)}=2\Delta,

(151)

Once more, it might be tempting to conclude that
\Delta
does not get renormalized because there are no diagrammatic contributions to the above term. This is in fact precisely the argument made by Chaté and Solon in
9
. However, this argument is not entirely correct. As we discuss in detail in Appendix
D
,
\Delta
does indeed get an anomalous dimension as per (
180
), and this can be traced to the peculiarities of any Lifshitz theory with
z\neq 1
.

We are now in a position to complete the calculation of the one-loop correction to the propagators. There are two diagrams of the following type that contribute to
M_{\psi\psi}
:

}}=M_{\psi\psi}^{(1)}.

(152)

However, these two diagrams are exactly the same except that their vertex factors have the opposite sign to each other. Therefore, they cancel and, since
M_{\psi\psi}^{(0)}=0
, we find that simply:

M_{\psi\psi}^{(0)}+M_{\psi\psi}^{(1)}=0.

(153)

Shifting our attention now to the one-loop correction to the vertices: Each vertex contains the same coupling constant,
\lambda
, and this factor is the same as the one appearing in the propagator. A priori, there is no reason why these should get renormalized uniformly, nor in the same manner as within the propagator. That is, the advection terms
\lambda{\mathbf{n}}(\psi)\cdot\nabla\psi
and
\lambda{\mathbf{n}}(\psi)\cdot\nabla\phi
could in principle get deformed into an arbitrary derivative expansion that need not resum into an analytic form, let alone the special trigonometric forms appearing in our theory. That it does to all loop orders is a very special property of our model, which is ultimately due to the nonlinear realization of the symmetries and field redundancies identified in Section
IV
. In operational terms, we can see this via the following argument: The only one-loop diagram that contributes to the renormalization of the
(2n+1)
-vertex
V_{2n+1}
is the
V_{2n+3}
vertex with two of the internal
\psi
lines contracted to form a loop. The external
\phi
field and
2n
out of the
2n+1
external
\psi
fields must be contracted with
2n+1
of the
2n+3
internal
\psi
fields. That gives a combinatoric factor of
\frac{1}{2}[(2n+3)!]
. The factor of
\frac{1}{2}
is the symmetry factor of the diagram. The result of the computation of the loop integral proceeds as before and gives the result
\mathscr{I}
. Since there is a relative minus sign between
V_{2n+3}
and
V_{2n+1}
, the one-loop contribution to
V_{2n+1}
also has a minus sign relative to
V_{2n+1}
itself. Finally, it is clear that this argument works equally well for
V_{2n+2}
, whose one-loop correction comes from the vertex
V_{2n+4}
by contracting two of the internal
\psi
fields to form a loop. Thus, letting
V
with no subscript stand for an arbitrary vertex, we find

V^{(0)}+V^{(1)}=\vbox{\hbox{

}}=(1-\mathscr{I})V^{(0)}.

(154)

Since
V^{(0)}
is proportional to
\lambda
, we find that result of the one-loop computation can be summarized by simply shifting
\lambda
in precisely the same manner in all of the vertex factors as well as in the propagator:

\lambda\rightarrow(1-\mathscr{I})\lambda.

(155)

We contrast this with the argument in
13
, which argues that the strict form of the vertices in terms of
\lambda
need not hold as one iterates the dynamical renormalization group treatment. Our computation shows that in fact it does, and is the result of a simple multiplicative renormalization of the coupling
\lambda
, which follows as a direct consequence of the non-linearly realized symmetries and field redundancies and associated Ward identities elaborated upon in Section
IV
. Were this not the case, the advection interaction potential would have been deformed into an arbitrary power series at any given loop order, subsequently destroying their trigonometric forms and, along with them, the interpretation of
\psi
as a flock angle and
{\mathbf{n}}(\psi)
as a flock director.

Before we move on to the discussion of higher orders in the loop expansion, we reiterate that, as will be elaborated upon in Appendix
D
and as given in (
180
),
\lambda
is not the only parameter that will end up getting renormalized.

Appendix C
Two- and Higher-Loop Order

We now move on to consider the effect of higher order loop corrections. From (
121
) and (
122
), one straightforwardly derives the relation

I=L-1+V,

(156)

where
V=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(V_{2n+1}+V_{2n+2})
is the total number of vertices, which is nothing more than a transcription of the fact that the Euler characteristic of the plane is 2, and that the Feynman diagrams in question are planar. The usual expression for the Euler characteristic is given by
18
18

18

The usage of
\chi
as the symbol for the Euler characteristic is standard. However, it should not be confused with the roughness exponent, which measures the scaling dimension of
\psi
, i.e.,
[\psi]=\chi
, nor with the original
\chi
field that we introduced in the MSRDJ procedure before converting to
\phi=-i\chi
.

\chi=V-E+F
, where
V
is the number of vertices,
E
the number of edges, and
F
the number of faces. We can re-express this in terms of
I
, the number of edges, and where
L+1
is the number of faces, given that we must count the rest of the plane outside the Feynman diagram as a face. Since
\chi=2-2g
, where
g
is the genus, and where
\chi=2
for the plane, so that one finally arrives at (
156
).

For
L=1
, we have
I=V
and so it is possible to have
I_{\phi\psi}=I
with each vertex having exactly one
\phi
field, except that the diagram would vanish by the nonrenormalization theorem discussed above. For
L=2
, however, this is not possible because
I=V+1
. Thus, the maximum value of
I_{\phi\psi}
is
I-1
. However, with two loops,
I_{\phi\psi}=I-1
will force one of the loops to be cyclic in
\phi
and
\psi
and thus vanish by the nonrenormalization theorem. Therefore, the maximum value for
I_{\phi\psi}
is in fact
I-2
. Consequently, the maximum superficial degree of divergence is

D_{\rm max}=8-4I+3(I-2)=2-I,

(157)

and so only
(I,I_{\phi\psi})=(2,0)
contributes. In other words, there is only one contributing diagram:

M_{ab}^{(2)}=\!\vbox{\hbox{

{}{{}}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-37.27165pt}{-2.15277pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{31.98576pt}{-3.47221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}},

(158)

where
a
and
b
can be
\phi
or
\psi
. This vanishes for
a=b=\phi
and
a=b=\psi
for the same reason that the one-loop diagrams vanish in those cases. Therefore,

\displaystyle M_{\psi\psi}^{(0)}+M_{\psi\psi}^{(1)}+M_{\psi\psi}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle M_{\phi\phi}^{(0)}+M_{\phi\phi}^{(1)}+M_{\phi\phi}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=2\Delta.

(159)

The result for
(a,b)=(\phi,\psi)
is thus

M_{\phi\psi}^{(2)}=\frac{1}{8}(2\mathscr{I})^{2}ik_{x}=\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{I}^{2}ik_{x}.

(160)

Compared to the one-loop diagram, which has a symmetry factor of 2, this diagram has a symmetry factor of
8=2^{L}L!
for
L=2
. The factor of
L!
comes from permutations of the loops, and where each loop comes with a factor of
2
since switching the two fields in each internal loop does not actually change the diagram. Therefore:

M_{\phi\psi}^{(0)}+M_{\phi\psi}^{(1)}+M_{\phi\psi}^{(2)}=-i\biggl[\omega-\biggl(1-\mathscr{I}+\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{I}^{2}\biggr)\lambda k_{x}+i\kappa k^{2}\biggr].

(161)

In other words,
\lambda
renormalizes to

\lambda\rightarrow\biggl(1-\mathscr{I}+\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{I}^{2}\biggr)\lambda.

(162)

By the same argument, the only diagram that contributes to the two-loop vertex correction is the one-loop diagram in (
163
) but with an extra loop added to the same vertex:

}}=\biggl(1-\mathscr{I}+\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{I}^{2}\biggr)V^{(0)}.

(163)

Thus, up to two-loop order, the result of the loop calculation is to shift
\lambda
in a way which is consistent across its appearance in the propagator and all of the interaction terms. However, again, this does not imply that
\lambda
is the
only
parameter that gets renormalized, as we’ll flesh out in the next section.

The pattern is clear: In general,
V=I-L+I
and so
(I_{\phi\psi})_{\rm max}=V=I-L+1
just from topological considerations. However, the nonrenormalization theorem implies that
(I_{\phi\psi})_{\rm max}
is one less than this, given that
(I_{\phi\psi})_{\rm max}=V=I-L
. Thus, the maximum superficial degree of divergence is

D_{\rm max}=4(L-I)+3(I_{\phi\psi})_{\rm max}=L-I.

(164)

Since
I\geq L
, the only possiblity is
I=L
, which is logarithmically divergent and has the structure of
L

\psi
-
\psi
loops attached to one vertex. Therefore, to all-loop order, the shift of
\lambda
reads

\lambda\rightarrow\sum_{L=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{L}}{2^{L}L!}(2\mathscr{I})^{L}\lambda=e^{-\mathscr{I}}\lambda=\biggl(\frac{\Lambda}{\mu}\biggr)^{-\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}}\lambda,

(165)

while there are no diagrammatic contributions to the
\Delta
or
\kappa
terms.

C.1
Random Phase Approximation

Instead of amputating the corrections to the propagators, we can instead sum all of the one-particle-irreducible (1PI) diagrams and then multiply each by two copies of the bare propagator. The sum of the amputated 1PI diagrams is the analog of the self energy
\Sigma
. For example,

\Sigma_{\phi\psi}\equiv\sum_{L=1}^{\infty}M_{\phi\psi}^{(L)}=\sum_{L=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-\mathscr{I})^{L}}{L!}i\lambda k_{x}=\bigl(e^{-\mathscr{I}}-1\bigr)i\lambda k_{x}=\biggl[\biggl(\frac{\Lambda}{\mu}\biggr)^{-\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}}-1\biggr]i\lambda k_{x}.

(166)

The 1PI correction to the propagator, as opposed to the inverse propagator, is given by the above flanked by one factor of the bare propagator on either side. The Random Phase Approximation (RPA) simply chains any number of these in series and sums them all up. Thus, the renormalized propagator is given by:

\displaystyle{\cal G}_{\phi\psi}^{\rm ren}

\displaystyle={\cal G}_{\phi\psi}^{(0)}+{\cal G}_{\phi\psi}^{(0)}\Sigma_{\phi\psi}{\cal G}_{\phi\psi}^{(0)}+{\cal G}_{\phi\psi}^{(0)}\Sigma_{\phi\psi}{\cal G}_{\phi\psi}^{(0)}\Sigma_{\phi\psi}{\cal G}_{\phi\psi}^{(0)}+\cdots

\displaystyle=\frac{{\cal G}_{\phi\psi}^{(0)}}{1-{\cal G}_{\phi\psi}^{(0)}\Sigma_{\phi\psi}}\equiv\frac{1}{\bigl({\cal G}_{\phi\psi}^{(0)}\bigr)^{-1}-\Sigma_{\phi\psi}},

which simplifies to

{\cal G}_{\phi\psi}^{\rm ren}=\frac{1}{i(\omega-\lambda k_{x}-i\kappa k^{2})-\bigl[\bigl(\frac{\Lambda}{\mu}\bigr)^{-\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}}-1\bigr]i\lambda k_{x}}=\frac{-i}{\omega-\bigl(\frac{\Lambda}{\mu}\bigr)^{-\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}}\lambda k_{x}-i\kappa k^{2}}.

(167)

Therefore, the renormalized propagator is the same as the original propagator with the
\lambda
parameter shifted as in (
165
). There are no diagrams with purely
\psi
internal loops that can contribute to
G_{\phi\phi}^{(0)}=0
, and so that persists in vanishing identically.

The corrections to the
\psi
-
\psi
propagator take the form of any number of corrections to the
\psi
-
\phi
propagator excluding the final bare
G_{\psi\phi}^{(0)}
, which is instead replaced with a
G_{\psi\psi}^{(0)}
insertion, followed by any number of corrections to the
\phi
-
\psi
propagator, excluding the initial bare
G_{\phi\psi}^{(0)}
, schematically depicted below, where the red solid line is the
G_{\psi\psi}^{(0)}
which replaces the
G_{\psi\phi}^{(0)}
, which would have been on the end of the left-hand portion of the diagram, and the
G_{\phi\psi}^{(0)}
, which would have been at the start of the right-hand portion of the diagram:

(168)

Again, there can be any number of RPA
G_{\psi\phi}
corrections on the left and RPA
G_{\phi\psi}
corrections on the right (and need not involve the same number on both sides).
Therefore, this evaluates to

{\cal G}_{\psi\psi}^{\rm ren}={\cal G}_{\psi\phi}^{\rm ren}\bigl({\cal G}_{\psi\phi}^{(0)}\bigr)^{-1}{\cal G}_{\psi\psi}^{(0)}\bigl({\cal G}_{\phi\psi}^{(0)}\bigr)^{-1}{\cal G}_{\phi\psi}^{\rm ren}.

(169)

Using the fact that

{\cal G}_{\psi\psi}^{(0)}=2\Delta{\cal G}_{\psi\phi}^{(0)}{\cal G}_{\phi\psi}^{(0)},

(170)

(
169
) simplifies to

{\cal G}_{\psi\psi}^{\rm ren}=2\Delta{\cal G}_{\psi\phi}^{\rm ren}{\cal G}_{\phi\psi}^{\rm ren},

(171)

which is indeed precisely the same as the bare propagator with
\lambda
shifted as in (
165
).

Once again, the complete all-loop calculation seems to naïvely indicate that the only quantity that gets renormalized is
\lambda
, which we know cannot be the case as this would violate the Ward identities discussed in Sec.
IV
. We finally turn towards this recurring point, along with the remaining details of the complete renormalization of the theory in the next section.

Appendix D
Wilsonian Renormalization

In what follows, we adopt the Wilsonian perspective on renormalization with hard cutoff regularization
19
19

19

Given the scheme-independence of the coefficients of the leading logarithmic UV divergences, along with the results of the previous subsections, one can also arrive at the same conclusions by isolating the UV poles via dimensional regularization and integrating the corresponding beta functions.
. We split the modes of the angle and response fields,
\psi
and
\phi
, into “slow” modes with momentum
<\frac{\Lambda}{b}
, where
\Lambda
is some UV scale and
b>1
is a real number, and “fast” modes with momentum between
\frac{\Lambda}{b}
and
\Lambda
. We integrate out the fast modes, which means that in the result (
165
), we set
\mu=\frac{\Lambda}{b}
so that the factor involving the cutoff becomes

\biggl(\frac{\Lambda}{\mu}\biggr)^{-\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}}\xrightarrow{\mu\rightarrow\Lambda/b}b^{-\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}}.

(172)

We then
rescale
the momentum back up so as to restore the domain of the slow modes to be between 0 and
\Lambda
. In so doing, we have to scale all dimensionful quantities according to their
bare
scaling dimension. The bare scaling dimensions have been determined in such a way as to force the action to be dimensionless, of course.

Though we performed the loop calculation in momentum space, we can, of course, have just as readily worked in real space, especially since the result was so simple: The renormalized action can at first glance be brought into a form that looks exactly the same as the bare action but with
\lambda
shifted according to (
165
), or, using (
172
), according to

\lambda\rightarrow b^{-r}\lambda,\qquad\qquad r\equiv\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}.

(173)

Denoting renormalized quantities with an overline, the results of our loop computation to all orders would seem to naïvely imply that

\displaystyle\overline{S}_{\rm free}

\displaystyle=-\int_{t,{\mathbf{x}}}\phi(t,{\mathbf{x}})\bigl[\partial_{t}+b^{-r}\lambda\partial_{x}+\kappa\nabla^{2}\bigr]\psi(t,{\mathbf{x}})-\Delta\int_{t,{\mathbf{x}}}\phi(t,{\mathbf{x}})^{2},

(174a)

\displaystyle\overline{S}_{\rm int}^{(2n+1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{n}}{(2n)!}b^{-r}\lambda\int_{t,{\mathbf{x}}}\psi(t,{\mathbf{x}})^{2n}\partial_{y}\phi(t,{\mathbf{x}}),

(174b)

\displaystyle\overline{S}_{\rm int}^{(2n+2)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{(-1)^{n}}{(2n+1)!}b^{-r}\lambda\int_{t,{\mathbf{x}}}\psi(t,{\mathbf{x}})^{2n+1}\partial_{x}\phi(t,{\mathbf{x}}).

(174c)

However, this would be incompatible with the Ward identities discussed in Section
IV
, which force the anomalous dimension of
\lambda
,
\Delta
, and
\kappa
to be the same at all loop orders. Those same Ward identities therefore imply that
r
must also remain dimensionless. Consistency of the renormalization of the
\phi
-
\psi
cross-terms in the action would then also necessitate that spatial derivatives (or spatial momenta) must themselves also gain anomalous dimensions as well. This latter fact may seem rather striking from a relativistic point of view, and warrants further elaboration. After all, in relativistic quantum field theory, one determines the wave function renormalization by assuming that any correction to the kinetic term must be absorbed entirely by the respective field and not by the spacetime derivatives. If this were not the case, there would be an ambiguity in how to cancel divergences (beyond the usual possibilities of regularization scheme-dependence). Of course, the point is that one is not allowed to alter the scaling of time or energy, because of the operational fact that renormalization is premised on separating out low-
energy
from high-
energy
modes, and integrating out the latter. In a relativistic theory, one cannot therefore change the scaling of space or momentum either.

On the other hand, in a theory with non-trivial Lifshitz scaling, an anomalous dimension for spatial momentum is implied by the renormalization of the dynamical critical exponent,
z
. Were it not possible for spatial momentum to gain an anomalous dimension, it would likewise be impossible for
z
to get renormalized. Therefore, given the preceding discussion, we are obliged to consider assigning anomalous dimensions to each of
\lambda
,
\Delta
,
\kappa
,
\phi
and
{\mathbf{x}}
, which we denote as
\gamma_{\lambda}
,
\gamma_{\Delta}
,
\gamma_{\kappa}
,
\gamma_{\phi}
, and
\gamma_{{\mathbf{x}}}
, respectively. That is, we define the renormalized parameters:

\displaystyle\overline{\lambda}

\displaystyle=b^{\gamma_{\lambda}}\lambda,

\displaystyle\overline{\Delta}

\displaystyle=b^{\gamma_{\Delta}}\Delta,

\displaystyle\overline{\kappa}

\displaystyle=b^{\gamma_{\kappa}}\kappa,

\displaystyle\overline{\phi}

\displaystyle=b^{\gamma_{\phi}}\phi,

\displaystyle\overline{{\mathbf{x}}}

\displaystyle=b^{\gamma_{{\mathbf{x}}}}{\mathbf{x}}.

(175)

As discussed above, the dimensions of time must remain unchanged, and
\psi
must remain exactly dimensionless in order for the trigonometric interactions to maintain their form, which is a consequence of the shift-rotation subgroup of the symmetry discussed in Section
IV
. That is,

\displaystyle\overline{t}

\displaystyle=t,\qquad\overline{\psi}=\psi.

(176)

Plugging in the above to express the bare quantities in (
174a
) in terms of renormalized quantities results in

\displaystyle\overline{S}_{\rm free}

\displaystyle=-b^{-2\gamma_{{\mathbf{x}}}-\gamma_{\phi}}\int_{\overline{t},\overline{{\mathbf{x}}}}\overline{\phi}(\overline{t},\overline{{\mathbf{x}}})\bigl[\partial_{\overline{t}}+b^{\gamma_{{\mathbf{x}}}-\gamma_{\lambda}-r}\overline{\lambda}\partial_{\overline{x}}+b^{2\gamma_{{\mathbf{x}}}-\gamma_{\kappa}}\overline{\kappa}\overline{\nabla}^{2}\bigr]\overline{\psi}(\overline{t},\overline{{\mathbf{x}}})

\displaystyle\quad-b^{-2\gamma_{{\mathbf{x}}}-2\gamma_{\phi}-\gamma_{\Delta}}\overline{\Delta}\int_{\overline{t},\overline{{\mathbf{x}}}}\overline{\phi}(\overline{t},\overline{{\mathbf{x}}})^{2},

(177)

which immediately implies the following relations:

\displaystyle\gamma_{\phi}

\displaystyle=-2\gamma_{{\mathbf{x}}},

\displaystyle\gamma_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{{\mathbf{x}}}-r,

\displaystyle\gamma_{\kappa}

\displaystyle=2\gamma_{{\mathbf{x}}},

\displaystyle\gamma_{\Delta}

\displaystyle=2\gamma_{{\mathbf{x}}}.

(178)

Note that consistency requires that
\Delta
and
\kappa
have the same anomalous dimension regardless of the anomalous dimension of space. Were this not the case, the ratio
r
would have failed to remain dimensionless. Again, this is protected by the Ward identities discussed in Section
IV
, which also provide the remaining relation,

\gamma_{\lambda}=\gamma_{\Delta}.

(179)

Solving these equations yields

\displaystyle\gamma_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{\Delta}=\gamma_{\kappa}=-\frac{\Delta}{2\pi\kappa},

\displaystyle\gamma_{\phi}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta}{2\pi\kappa},

\displaystyle\gamma_{{\mathbf{x}}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}.

(180)

The renormalized value of
z
is obtained from the ratio of the fully dressed scaling dimensions of the temporal and spatial coordinates, and is therefore given by:

\overline{z}=\frac{[\bar{t}]}{[\bar{x}]}=\frac{-2}{-1-\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}}=\frac{2}{1+\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}}.

(181)

Since
\Delta
and
\kappa
should be positive, the dynamical critical exponent is indeed less than 2
13
.

We note that in
9
;
13
, the authors make the a priori reasonable distinction between the factor
\lambda
that appears in the
\phi
-
\psi
and
\psi
-
\phi
propagators and the factor of
\lambda
that appears in the interaction vertices. In
13
, for example, the former is called
\lambda_{1}
and the latter is called
-\lambda_{2}
. The reason for this distinction is that although
\lambda_{1}=-\lambda_{2}=\lambda
at the bare level, loop corrections will generically spoil this relation
20
20

20

The authors go on to argue that there can be no nontrivial fixed point with
\lambda_{1}\neq 0
and
\lambda_{2}=0
or with
\lambda_{1}=0
and
\lambda_{2}\neq 0
. That is, any fixed point must have
\lambda_{1}\neq 0
and
\lambda_{2}\neq 0
, which is consistent with our findings.
. The only manner in which this relation can be preserved is if it is somehow protected by symmetry. As demonstrated in Section
IV
, this protection is in fact provided by a non-linear realization of the one-parameter shift/rotation subgroup in (
) that forces relations between the various vertex factors at all loops, such that the recursion relations (
21
) hold to all orders, and the trigonometric form of the advection interaction is invariant under RG up to a rescaling of
\lambda
. That is, all appearances of
\lambda
, whether in the propagators or in the vertex factors are forced to renormalize in the exact same manner because of this symmetry.

Therefore, indeed, it is true that there cannot be nontrivial fixed point unless both
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
are nonzero, and in fact,
\lambda_{1}=-\lambda_{2}\equiv\lambda
. However, it is not possible to further specify the precise value of
\lambda
at any fixed point or collection of fixed points unless one specifies some quantity that depends on
\lambda
at some particular scale,
\bar{\mu}
to fix the value of
\lambda
at that scale as a renormalization condition that subsequently allows us to infer the value of
\lambda
at any other scale,
\mu
. Doing so, results in the scale dependent coupling

\lambda(\mu)=\biggl(\frac{\bar{\mu}}{\mu}\biggr)^{1-\frac{\Delta}{2\pi\kappa}}\lambda(\bar{\mu}).

(182)

Which can be viewed as the result of having integrated the corresponding beta function:

\mu\frac{d\lambda}{d\mu}=-\biggl(1-\frac{\Delta}{2\pi\kappa}\biggr)\lambda.

(183)

It is more typical to express RG running in terms of an RG scale
s
, which flows from the UV to the IR (thus encapsulating that it is a monotonic flow resulting from successive coarse graining over short distance modes), and so
increases
as
\mu
decreases. Hence,

\frac{d\lambda}{ds}=\biggl(1-\frac{\Delta}{2\pi\kappa}\biggr)\lambda,

(184)

where
s=\log(\bar{\mu}/\mu)
. We stress in closing this appendix that common to all field theoretic computations, there is a degree of arbitrariness with respect to field redefinitions (or changes of variable such that the transformed variable also represents a sufficient statistic) that will propagate onto how individual couplings that parameterize individual operators in the action renormalize. All of this arbitrariness will drop out of physical observables, which is the content of the equivalence theorem for on-shell S-matrix elements in quantum field theory
7
;
40
, but is much more simply the invariance of the measure and generating functional for connected correlation functions in statistical field theories up to a suitable redefinition of the source
21
;
44
. The final result of our all-loop resummation of the isotropic Malthusian flock is to have elucidated its phase structure, and this will have resulted no matter the particular scheme or operator basis one may have chosen to expand in.

Appendix E
Symmetries and Invariances of Mathusian Flocks

We observe that the MSRDJ action for the Malthusian flock has a very special structure, by first noting that the functional
\mathcal{E}
in Eq. (
4
) can be written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}_{\psi},

\displaystyle j_{\psi}^{t}

\displaystyle=\psi,

\displaystyle\mathbf{j}_{\psi}

\displaystyle=-\kappa\nabla\psi-\lambda\hat{\mathbf{n}}_{\perp}.

(185)

Thus, the MSRDJ Lagrangian takes the form

{\cal L}=\phi\,\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}_{\psi}-\Delta\phi^{2}.

(186)

Integrating the above by parts indicates that the current
j^{\mu}_{\psi}
couples only to
\partial_{\mu}\phi
, and so it is natural to consider the set of all possible redundanciens of the MSRDJ action under constant shifts of the response field.

Therefore, we consider a transformation of the coordinates with the corresponding transformation of
\phi
and
j^{\mu}_{\psi}
in addition to inhomogeneous contributions:

\displaystyle x^{\mu}

\displaystyle\rightarrow x^{\prime\mu}=x^{\mu}+\epsilon^{\mu}(x),

(187a)

\displaystyle\phi(x)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\phi^{\prime}(x^{\prime})=\phi(x)+c,

(187b)

\displaystyle j^{\mu}_{\psi}(x)

\displaystyle\rightarrow j_{\psi^{\prime}}^{\prime\mu}(x^{\prime})=\frac{\partial x^{\prime\mu}}{\partial x^{\nu}}j^{\nu}_{\psi}(x)+\omega^{\mu}(x)=j^{\mu}_{\psi}(x)+\frac{\partial\epsilon^{\mu}}{\partial x^{\nu}}j^{\nu}_{\psi}(x)+\omega^{\mu}(x),

(187c)

where
\omega^{\mu}
is an as yet to be determined contribution induced by transformations of
\psi
that we aim to solve for by demanding that the action be invariant. We furthermore assume that
\epsilon^{\mu}
,
c
, and
\omega^{\mu}
are infinitesimal and we keep only up to linear order in these quantities. Then,
\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}_{\psi}
transforms as

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}_{\psi}(x)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\partial^{\prime}_{\mu}j_{\psi^{\prime}}^{\prime\mu}(x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial x^{\rho}}{\partial x^{\prime\nu}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\rho}}\biggl(\frac{\partial x^{\prime\nu}}{\partial x^{\mu}}j_{\psi}^{\mu}(x)+\omega^{\nu}(x)\biggr)

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}_{\psi}(x)+\frac{\partial^{2}\epsilon^{\nu}}{\partial x^{\nu}\partial x^{\mu}}j_{\psi}^{\mu}(x)+\partial_{\mu}\omega^{\mu}(x).

(188)

Therefore, the Lagrangian transforms as

\displaystyle{\cal L}\rightarrow{\cal L}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\phi^{\prime}(x^{\prime})\,\partial_{\mu}^{\prime}j_{\psi^{\prime}}^{\prime\mu}(x^{\prime})-\Delta\phi^{\prime}(x^{\prime})^{2}

\displaystyle=\bigl[\phi(x)+c\bigr]\biggl(\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}_{\psi}(x)+\frac{\partial^{2}\epsilon^{\nu}}{\partial x^{\nu}\partial x^{\mu}}j^{\mu}_{\psi}(x)+\partial_{\mu}\omega^{\mu}(x)\biggr)-\Delta\bigl[\phi(x)+c\bigr]^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}+c\,\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}_{\psi}+\phi\biggl(\partial_{\mu}\omega^{\mu}+\frac{\partial^{2}\epsilon^{\nu}}{\partial x^{\nu}\partial x^{\mu}}j^{\mu}_{\psi}(x)-2\Delta c\biggr).

(189)

Since
c
is a constant, the term
c\,\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}_{\psi}
is a total derivative. Thus, the action is invariant if

\partial_{\mu}\omega^{\mu}=2\Delta c-(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\epsilon^{\nu})j^{\mu}_{\psi}.

(190)

Consider the coordinate transformation given by an anti-clockwise rotation of the Cartesian coordinates by a time-dependent and spatially-independent angle
\theta(t)
:

\displaystyle t^{\prime}

\displaystyle=t,

\displaystyle x^{\prime}

\displaystyle=x-y\theta,

\displaystyle y^{\prime}

\displaystyle=y+x\theta.

(191)

Note that
\partial_{\mu}\epsilon^{\mu}=0
for this transformation, and so the condition (
190
) simplifies to
\partial_{\mu}\omega^{\mu}=2\Delta c
.

One could also consider independent shifts of each coordinate as well as Galilean boost, but we will not need it here. On the one hand,

\displaystyle j_{\psi^{\prime}}^{\prime t}

\displaystyle=j^{t}_{\psi}+\omega^{t},

(192a)

\displaystyle j_{\psi^{\prime}}^{\prime x}

\displaystyle=j^{x}_{\psi}-\theta j^{y}_{\psi}-y\dot{\theta}j^{t}_{\psi}+\omega^{x}=j^{x}_{\psi}-\theta(-\kappa\partial_{y}\psi-\lambda\cos\psi)-y\dot{\theta}\psi+\omega^{x},

(192b)

\displaystyle j_{\psi^{\prime}}^{\prime y}

\displaystyle=j^{y}_{\psi}+\theta j^{x}_{\psi}+x\dot{\theta}j^{t}_{\psi}+\omega^{y}=j^{y}_{\psi}+\theta(-\kappa\partial_{x}\psi+\lambda\sin\psi)+x\dot{\theta}\psi+\omega^{y}.

(192c)

On the other hand, Eq. (
192a
) above, along with
j^{t}_{\psi}=\psi(x)
, and
j_{\psi^{\prime}}^{\prime t}=\psi^{\prime}(x^{\prime})
implies that

\psi^{\prime}(x^{\prime})=\psi(x)+\omega^{t}(x),

(193)

so that the time component of
\omega^{\mu}
by definition is the transformation of the flock angle, so that the field transformations are given by

\delta\psi\equiv\psi^{\prime}(x^{\prime})-\psi(x)=\omega^{t}(x),\qquad\delta\phi\equiv\phi^{\prime}(x^{\prime})-\phi(x)=c.

(194)

Therefore, using the spatial components of
j^{\mu}
in Eq. (
185
), substituting the above, and expanding to first order:

\displaystyle j^{\prime x}_{\psi^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=-\kappa\partial_{x^{\prime}}\psi^{\prime}+\lambda\sin\psi^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-\kappa(\partial_{x}-\theta\partial_{y})(\psi+\omega^{t})+\lambda\sin(\psi+\omega^{t})

\displaystyle=-\kappa\partial_{x}\psi+\kappa\theta\partial_{y}\psi-\kappa\partial_{x}\omega^{t}+\lambda\sin\psi+\lambda\omega^{t}\cos\psi

\displaystyle=j^{x}_{\psi}+\kappa\theta\partial_{y}\psi-\kappa\partial_{x}\omega^{t}+\lambda\omega^{t}\cos\psi,

(195)

and

\displaystyle j^{\prime y}_{\psi^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=-\kappa\partial_{y^{\prime}}\psi^{\prime}-\lambda\cos\psi^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-\kappa(\partial_{y}+\theta\partial_{x})(\psi+\omega^{t})-\lambda\cos(\psi+\omega^{t})

\displaystyle=-\kappa\partial_{y}\psi-\kappa\theta\partial_{x}\psi-\kappa\partial_{y}\omega^{t}-\lambda\cos\psi+\lambda\omega^{t}\sin\psi

\displaystyle=j^{y}_{\psi}-\kappa\theta\partial_{x}\psi-\kappa\partial_{y}\omega^{t}+\lambda\omega^{t}\sin\psi.

(196)

Comparing with Eq. (
192b
) and (
192c
), one can solve for
\omega^{x}
and
\omega^{y}
in terms of
\omega^{t}
, which we redefine for convenience as
\omega^{t}\equiv\delta\psi:=W
, so that

\displaystyle\omega^{t}

\displaystyle=W,

(197a)

\displaystyle\omega^{x}

\displaystyle=-\kappa\partial_{x}W+\lambda(W-\theta)\cos\psi+y\dot{\theta}\psi,

(197b)

\displaystyle\omega^{y}

\displaystyle=-\kappa\partial_{y}W+\lambda(W-\theta)\sin\psi-x\dot{\theta}\psi.

(197c)

Taking the divergence of the above, and imposing the condition that guarantees the invariance of the action (
190
), implies that
W\equiv\delta\psi
must satisfy:

\partial_{\mu}\omega^{\mu}=\dot{W}-\kappa\nabla^{2}W+\lambda\nabla\cdot\bigl[(W-\theta)\hat{\mathbf{n}}(\psi)\bigr]+\dot{\theta}(y\partial_{x}-x\partial_{y})\psi=2\Delta c,

(198)

which can be re-expressed as

\dot{W}-\kappa\nabla^{2}W+\lambda\hat{\textbf{n}}(\psi)\cdot\nabla W+\lambda W\nabla\cdot\hat{\textbf{n}}(\psi)=2\Delta c+i\dot{\theta}L\psi+\lambda\theta\nabla\cdot\hat{\textbf{n}}(\psi).

(199)

As a first order PDE (convection-diffusion), one can always obtain a unique solution given a fixed set of boundary conditions
3
;
32
. That is, demanding that
\phi\to\phi+c
along with making an infinitesimal time-dependent rotation implies that there will always be a unique solution to (
199
)
W
so that
\psi\to\psi+W(t,x,y)
leaves the action invariant.

One can readily check that the symmetry corresponding to a constant shift in
\psi
along with a constant rotation (i.e.,
c=0
,
\theta(t)=\nu
,
W=\nu
), solves (
199
). One can also consider the case where
\theta=0
, in which case a transformation
\phi\rightarrow\phi+c
and
\psi\rightarrow\psi+W
is guaranteed to exist, and therefore also leaves the action invariant and thus corresponds to a physically indistinguishable configuration. Clearly one can proceed and consider more elaborate spacetime transformations in addition to constant shifts in
\phi
and
solve
for the shift
W
that would leave the action invariant, which defines the transformation on the flock angle through the identification
W\equiv\delta\psi
.

Appendix F
Comparisons with Previous Treatments of Flock Scaling

The recent literature presents the interested reader with differing viewpoints regarding the extent to which symmetry arguments constrain the scaling behavior of two-dimensional polar flocks
9
;
25
;
13
;
10
;
12
;
11
. Since our treatment deploys an explicit diagrammatic renormalization procedure, it is useful to summarize the assumptions underlying the approaches of
9
;
10
;
11
relative to
13
;
12
, and to clarify where and how our conclusions might differ. In addition, while the debate between the two aforementioned sets of authors has dominated the discussion in this area, a notable alternative viewpoint has been proposed by Amoretti et al.
2
, which utilizes boost-agnostic hydrodynamics and thermodynamic constraints to derive a different set of relations among the critical exponents.

F.1
Chaté–Solon: Symmetry-Based Non-Renormalization Arguments

In
9
, Chaté and Solon analyze the Goldstone-mode dynamics of the ordered phase and argue that certain nonlinear couplings do not get renormalized. In particular, immediately following their Eq. (10), they state that the terms originating from the convective derivative are not expected to receive graphical corrections. In their formulation, these terms are proportional to the parameter
\lambda
, and the argument is based on the generalized Galilean invariance introduced in their Appendix B. Our findings lead us to a different conclusion. Our explicit diagrammatic analysis finds nonvanishing renormalization of the couplings associated with the convective derivative. Consequently, the non-renormalization of these couplings does not appear to follow from the symmetries realized in our formulation, which moreover, protects their functional form under loop corrections. It is worth noting that the generalized Galilean invariance invoked in Ref.
9
is derived within a theory truncated at a particular order in the expansions of
\sin\psi
and
\cos\psi
and is evidently not a symmetry of the full non-linear theory, as elaborated upon in Section
IV
. Whether an analogous symmetry survives in a more general treatment is a separate question. However, regardless of its interpretation, our diagrammatic calculation indicates that the corresponding couplings acquire nonzero anomalous dimensions.

A second point concerns the variance of the noise
\Delta
. Chaté and Solon argue that because all interactions are derivatively coupled,
\Delta
receives no graphical corrections and therefore acquires no anomalous dimension. We agree with the first statement: in our calculation,
\Delta
indeed receives no direct diagrammatic corrections. However, we arrive at a different conclusion regarding the second statement. Even in the absence of direct graphical corrections, the scaling dimension of
\Delta
can be modified through its coupling to other running parameters, including the nonlinear couplings, and this is due to certain subtleties of the renormalization procedure in boost non-invariant theories with non-trivial Lifshitz scaling (i.e. with
z\neq 1
), as discussed in Appendix
D
. Thus, the absence of explicit diagrams contributing to
\Delta
does not, by itself, imply that it does not acquire an anomalous dimension.

These assumptions lead directly to the exponent relations proposed in
9
. For Malthusian flocks, Chaté and Solon derive three relations among the scaling exponents
(\chi,\zeta,z)
, from which they obtain exact exponent values. Their first relation,

2\chi+z-\zeta=0,

(200)

follows from the claim that the coupling
\lambda_{x}
multiplying the interaction
\lambda_{x}\partial_{x}(\psi^{3})
receives no anomalous dimension. Their second relation,

\chi+z-1=0,

(201)

follows from the analogous claim for the coupling
\lambda_{y}
multiplying
\lambda_{y}\partial_{y}\psi^{2}
. In both cases, the justification is the generalized Galilean invariance discussed in Appendix B of Ref.
9
. Their third relation,

z-2\chi-1-\zeta=0,

(202)

follows from the assumption that
\Delta
acquires no anomalous dimension because no diagrams contribute directly to its renormalization. Thus, they derive the critical exponents

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=\frac{5}{4},

\displaystyle\zeta

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{4},

\displaystyle\chi

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4},

(203)

with which we clearly differ.

The generalized Galilean invariance employed in Ref.
9
is expressed through their Eq. (B1),

\psi^{\prime}(\mathbf{r},t)=\psi(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{r}_{c},t)+\psi_{0},

(204)

with

\partial_{t}x_{c}=2\lambda_{x}\psi_{0},\qquad\partial_{t}y_{c}=\lambda_{y}\psi_{0}.

(205)

Since the symmetry structure adopted in the present work differs from that used in Ref.
9
, and moreover, is valid for the full non-linear theory, it is perhaps unsurprising that our renormalization analysis does not support the resulting non-renormalization conditions for
\lambda_{x}
and
\lambda_{y}
.

F.2
Chen
et al.
: Limitations of Symmetry Constraints and Dynamical RG

In
13
, the authors revisit the derivation of exact exponent relations. Their principal conclusion is that, for Malthusian flocks, only two independent exponent relations can be established. Consequently, they argue that the three critical exponents cannot be determined exactly. An important point is that the disagreement between Chen
et al.
and Chaté–Solon is narrower than it may initially appear. Both groups agree on two exponent relations. Indeed, one of the relations obtained by Chen
et al.
is equivalent to the difference between the first two equations in Eq. (11) of
9
,

\chi-\zeta+1=0.

(206)

The primary disagreement concerns the existence of a third independent relation. Chen
et al.
also accept that
\Delta
receives no direct graphical corrections and therefore recover the relation

z-2\chi-1-\zeta=0

(207)

using essentially the same reasoning as Chaté and Solon. Their departure from
9
instead concerns the nonlinear couplings. Rather than arguing that
\lambda_{x}
and
\lambda_{y}
have vanishing anomalous dimensions, Chen
et al.
argue only that their anomalous dimensions must be equal because both couplings originate from the same underlying parameter
\lambda
. Equating the anomalous dimensions of
\lambda_{x}
and
\lambda_{y}
yields a single exponent relation, whereas setting both anomalous dimensions individually to zero (as done in
9
) yields two. This difference is ultimately responsible for the differing conclusions regarding the exact determination of the Malthusian-flock exponents.

For immortal flocks, the disagreement becomes more substantial. Chen
et al.
argue that the currently known symmetries of flocking theories are insufficient to derive any exact relations among the scaling exponents. Consequently, they conclude that no exact exponent values can presently be inferred from symmetry considerations alone. Chaté and Solon, while not explicitly deriving exact exponent relations for immortal flocks, argue that the renormalization-group treatment of Chen
et al.
does not establish such a no-go theorem and therefore does not exclude the possibility that additional symmetry-based constraints may exist.

F.3
Amoretti et al.: Leveraging Thermodynamic Relations

In
2
, the authors make use of thermodynamic relations among the various constants appearing in the hydrodynamic equations (the generalized continuity equation and Euler equation, corresponding to their equations 13a and 13b, respectively) to derive two critical-exponent relations, which are distinct from those of the previous two sets of authors. This argument proceeds by first relating all of these coefficients to parameters and transport coefficients of boost-agnostic hydrodynamics and then claiming that these relations should be preserved under RG. The relevant constants split into two sets of like scaling dimension and setting these dimensions to 0 gives the two relations

\displaystyle z-1+\chi

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle z-\zeta+\chi

\displaystyle=0.

(208)

By the same argument as in the other works, namely that
\Delta
gains no anomous dimenion, these authors again get the relation

z-2\chi+1-d-\zeta=0,

(209)

where
d
is the number of spatial dimensions. This relation reduces to Eq. (
202
) and (
207
) of the previous two works when
d=2
. Thus, their critical exponents are

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{3},

\displaystyle\zeta

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle\chi=-\frac{1}{3}.

(210)

We agree with
\zeta=1
, but not with the other two critical exponents.

It should be noted that this work utilizes the generalized continuity equation. That is the evolution equation for density perturbations, which should be ignored for Malthusian flocks. In fact, five out of the eight coefficients considered in this work involve density fluctuations and should be removed in the Malthusian theory. Of the eight coefficients that they consider, only two coefficients of dimension
z-1+\chi
(their
g_{4}
and
g_{5}
) and one coefficient of dimension
z-\zeta+\chi
(their
w_{3}
) do not multiply terms involving the density fluctuation. However,
w_{3}
is a coefficient appearing in the evolution equation of the density fluctuation. Therefore, if that is to be dropped altogether for Malthusian flocks, then only their relation
z-1+\chi=0
would remain. Note that this would be consistent with Eq. (
201
) of Chaté and Solon, though it would lack a third equation to completely determine the values of the critical exponents.

F.4
Relation to this Work

The present analysis differs from these three approaches in that it is based on a different set of symmetries and field redundancies as those considered in
9
and a different set of renormalization conditions, particularly as pertains to the
\Delta
parameter, as in all three approaches. We back up the RG predictions derived from the associated Ward identities with an explicit diagrammatic renormalization procedure. Of course, we cannot claim to arrive at any firm conclusions beyond the confines of the very symmetric limit in which we have worked, and much more work remains to be done before we can address any of the relevant questions away from this limit.
